We need to prove strong normalization for CBV STLC with if-then-else. The trick: CBV reduction is deterministic-ish. Actually for STLC CBV, we can use a simple logical relation: a term is SN if all its reduction paths are finite.

The standard approach for strong normalization with CBV: define the logical relation on closed terms:
- For Ty_Bool: SN t (since Bool has no closed values other than true/false... actually closed terms of type Bool).
- For arrow: SN t ∧ ∀ v, value v → ⟦T1⟧ v → ⟦T2⟧ (t v).

Hmm, but the standard "reducibility" for CBV in Girard's style needs closure under reduction: if t reduces to t' and t' is reducible, then t is reducible (for SN, this is automatic: SN t is defined via strongly_normalizing).

Actually, the simplest approach: just prove that for closed well-typed terms, `strongly_normalizing t`. The key lemmas:
- If t is SN and t --> t', then t' is SN. Easy from SN_intro (inversion).
- If all t' with t --> t' are SN, then t is SN (that's SN_intro).
- Preservation + progress gives weak normalization but not strong... Hmm, actually, with the fact that step is deterministic? Is CBV step deterministic here? Let's check: ST_App1 requires t1 --> t1' and locally_closed t2; ST_App2 requires value v1. These are mutually exclusive: if t1 = tm_abs T t, value v1, then ST_AppAbs applies for t2... but also ST_App2 applies when t2 --> t2'. So ST_AppAbs and ST_App2 could both apply to tm_app (tm_abs T t) t2 when t2 is not a value. Indeed: tm_app (tm_abs T t) t2 where t2 not a value: ST_AppAbs doesn't apply since t2 not value; ST_App2 applies if t2 reduces. Also ST_App1 applies? t1 = tm_abs T t is a value, can it step? A value cannot step (abs requires locally_closed; abs doesn't reduce). So t1 --> t1' is false for values. So deterministic indeed at the app level. Hmm, but strong normalization could be proven via a simpler measure if deterministic: any term of a given type has bounded reduction length. But that's still a logical-relation style argument (the standard "SN implies... ").

**Design: LR over "sigma-substituted" terms, with an abstraction lemma.**

Define `LR T t` for arbitrary t (as above, where SN is over the whole term language).

Define `sem_ctx Gamma sigma := forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> LR T (sigma x)`.

Define `satisfies Gamma t T := forall sigma, sem_ctx Gamma sigma -> LR T (subst sigma t)`.

Prove by induction on the typing derivation that `has_type Gamma t T -> satisfies Gamma t T`.

- T_Var: `subst sigma (tm_fvar x) = sigma x`, and Gamma x = Some T, so sem_ctx gives LR T (sigma x). ✓.
- T_True/T_False: LR Bool (tm_true) = SN tm_true — need subst: subst sigma tm_true = tm_true, and SN tm_true. ✓
- T_App: IH1 gives LR (T1→T2) (subst sigma t1), IH2 gives LR T1 (subst sigma t2); apply the application lemma. ✓
- T_If: IH1: LR Bool (subst sigma t1) = SN (subst sigma t1). IH2, IH3: LR T (subst sigma t2/t3). Need LR T (subst sigma (tm_if t1 t2 t3)) = LR T (tm_if (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2) (subst sigma t3)). Need an "if lemma": if SN t1, LR T t2, LR T t3, then LR T (tm_if t1 t2 t3). This requires the "expanding"/"neutral" lemma: the if term reduces only via t1 stepping, or t1 = true/false reducing to t2/t3 (which are in LR). This is where we need the generalized lemma: "if all one-step reducts of t are in LR T and t is SN (or the reducts are all SN and the term is 'stuck-compatible'), then LR T t".

  Since LR T includes SN as a component, we could define a general "LR closure" property: 
  `Lemma LR_step_back : forall T t t', LR T t -> t --> t' -> LR T t'` (needs locally_closed? ST_App1 needs lc t2 — handled as noted).
  Then for the if lemma: we show LR T (if t1 t2 t3) by proving SN (if t1 t2 t3) and the arrow component.
  - SN: since t1, t2, t3 all SN (t2, t3 from LR → SN), every step of if t1 t2 t3 leads to a term which is SN: if t1 --> t1', we get if t1' t2 t3 where t1' is SN and t2, t3 SN... circular, need a nested induction: SN of the whole if-term given SN t1, SN t2, SN t3. This is provable by induction on SN t1, then on SN t2 / SN t3 as needed. Let me think: The reducts are: if t1' t2 t3 (from t1 step), t2 (if t1 = true), t3 (if t1 = false). We can prove by induction on (SN t1, SN t2, SN t3): 
    - Actually SN (if t1 t2 t3) given SN t1: induction on SN t1 derivation; for each reduct: if it's if t1' t2 t3 with t1 --> t1', then t1' is SN (from SN t1's premise), and we can apply the IH (which requires SN t1', SN t2, SN t3) — that works with IH being `forall t1', t1 --> t1' -> SN t1' -> ...` hmm, standard nested induction: 
    ```
    Lemma SN_if : forall t1 t2 t3, SN t1 -> SN t2 -> SN t3 -> SN (tm_if t1 t2 t3).
    Proof.
      intros t1 t2 t3 H1. revert t2 t3. induction H1 as [t1 H1 IH]. intros t2 t3 H2 H3.
      apply SN_intro. intros t' Hstep. inversion Hstep; subst.
      - (* ST_If *) apply IH; [assumption | assumption | assumption].  -- need H1 for the specific t1' 
      ...
    ```
    Wait, `SN_intro` for t1 gives `forall t1', t1 --> t1' -> SN t1'` and IH: `forall t1', t1 --> t1' -> forall t2 t3, SN t2 -> SN t3 -> SN (if t1' t2 t3)`. Good.

Actually here's the classic slick approach: define LR by recursion on T, with:
- LR Bool t := SN t
- LR (A→B) t := forall v, value v -> LR A v -> LR B (t v)
and then prove separately `LR T t -> SN t` by induction on T:

OK so here's the emerging structure, which is the standard one:

1. Define LR (type-indexed, on terms) with SN conjunct for arrows.

**Better approach**: Prove the application lemma directly by well-founded induction on SN derivations, using the structure:

Lemma LR_app : forall A B t1 t2, LR (A→B) t1 -> LR A t2 -> LR B (t1 t2).

Proof attempt: induction on SN derivation of t1, then... but we also need to handle t2 stepping. Let's think about what's needed for LR B (t1 t2):
(i) SN (t1 t2): 
   - t1 is SN, t2 is SN (from LR A t2 → SN t2).
   - Prove SN_app: SN t1 -> SN t2 -> SN (t1 t2). Induction on SN t1, then on SN t2? Let's see the reducts of t1 t2:

     ```
     Lemma SN_app : forall t1 t2, SN t1 -> SN t2 -> SN (tm_app t1 t2).
     Proof.
       intros t1 t2 H1. revert t2. induction H1 as [t1 Hreds IH]. intros t2 H2.
       induction H2 as [t2 Hreds2 IH2].
       apply SN_intro. intros t' Hstep. inversion Hstep; subst.

     Uh oh: the AppAbs case gives `open e t2`, which need not be covered by the induction hypotheses. **Determinism + measure approach**: Define the "size" of a term: number of nodes. Does each CBV step decrease the size? Let me instead consider the standard **Girard/Tait logical relation adapted for CBV** and just be careful with the closure lemma. I think it's manageable. Let me carefully design it.

Define:
```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => SN t /\ (forall v, value v -> LR T1 v -> LR T2 (tm_app t v))
  end.
```

Note LR T t -> SN t always (for Bool trivially, for arrow first component). But wait: for the arrow case, LR T1 v is required — for v a value, SN v holds, fine.

**Goal 1: substitution/opening lemmas.** Since LR is defined on terms with fvars/bvars, we need to handle the fundamental theorem. Let me think about whether to use general substitution or opening with fvars.

Option A (general substitution `subst sigma`): We need to show that substituting LR terms for fvars preserves LR. The fundamental theorem: Gamma |-- t \in T and sem_ctx Gamma sigma implies LR T (subst sigma t). For T_Abs: need LR (T1→T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)). By LR_abs lemma: need for all values v with LR T1 v: LR T2 (open (subst sigma t1) v). Now, the typing IH applies to `open t1 (tm_fvar x)` for fresh x, with the extended context `x |-> T1 ; Gamma` and extended sigma `sigma_x := fun y => if x == y then v else sigma y`. Then `subst sigma_x (open t1 (tm_fvar x))` should equal `open (subst sigma t1) v`... but this requires a lemma about subst/open commutation, which in locally nameless requires that x is not free in t1 (which holds if x is fresh enough: x not in the free fvars of t1 and not... yes, T_Abs provides L, and we choose x ∉ L, and also x should not be in the "domain" issues of sigma — but sigma is arbitrary, so we need x not free in t1; then `subst sigma_x (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) v`? Let's check: opening t1 with fvar x replaces bvar 0 occurrences with fvar x; then subst replaces fvar x with v and other fvars y with sigma y. On the other side, subst sigma t1 replaces fvars with sigma, leaving bvars; then open with v replaces bvar 0 with v. These agree **provided t1 has no fvar x occurring free** (otherwise LHS would replace those occurrences of x with v, but RHS keeps them as fvar x then... wait RHS: subst sigma t1 replaces fvar x with sigma x, and then opening replaces bvar 0 with v. LHS: opening replaces bvar 0 with fvar x, then subst replaces fvar x with v — including the original fvar x occurrences! So mismatch if t1 contains fvar x. So need x fresh for t1). Good, T_Abs gives "forall x, ~ In x L -> ...", and L can be chosen to contain all fvars of t1... but we don't have a lemma giving the list of fvars of t1. Hmm, we'd need to construct such an L. We can prove: for any t1, there is a list L containing all its free fvars. Or use a "pick fresh" lemma with a list of all atoms occurring in t1 plus L. That's doable but heavy.

Actually the standard locally nameless approach avoids general substitution and instead uses this key idea: the fundamental theorem is stated with a context interpretation that maps fvars to terms, but the abstraction case uses the "open with fresh fvar" and then instantiates the environment to map that fresh fvar to the value v. That still needs the commutation lemma above. Alternatively, prove the fundamental theorem directly in terms of **opening with fvars** and use an environment that is defined on the fly:

Actually here's the standard approach used in Charguéraud's locally nameless tutorial for STLC (the "typing" relation with `open`): Define semantic typing as follows:

```
Definition env := atom -> option ty.   (* given *)
Definition env_sem (E : context) (rho : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, E x = Some T -> LR T (rho x).
```
and prove
```
Lemma typing_sound : forall E t T, E |-- t \in T ->
  forall rho, env_sem E rho -> LR T (subst rho t).
```
with the subst/open commutation lemma. I think this is fine, we just need to develop the required infrastructure:
- `subst` function,
- `subst_open` lemma: for x fresh for t (i.e., x not free in t), `subst (extend rho x v) (open t (fvar x)) = open (subst rho t) v`. Hmm, but actually we need a generalized version because opening under binders shifts indices... In locally nameless, `open t (fvar x)` opens only index 0. Under a nested abstraction, index 1 is not opened by `open`, only by `open_rec 1`. Note `open_rec k u t` opens index k. In the T_Abs rule, `open t1 (tm_fvar x)` opens index 0 of t1. When we go under a nested binder within t1, the rule applies again at the inner level... The typing derivation handles this: for the nested abstraction inside t1, the next T_Abs rule applies to its body with `open t2 (fvar y)` which opens index 0 of t2. So all openings are at index 0 of the respective subterm. So subst/open commutation for index 0 openings should suffice, with the freshness condition being about the specific subterm being opened.

Let's define:
```
Definition subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm := ...
```
and prove:
```
Lemma subst_open_fvar : forall sigma t x v,
  ~ In x (fvars t) ->    (* or some freshness *)
  subst (fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else sigma y) (open t (tm_fvar x))
    = open (subst sigma t) v.
```
Hmm wait: `open t (fvar x)` replaces bvar 0 with fvar x, and leaves other bvars. `subst sigma t` leaves bvars. Then `open (subst sigma t) v` replaces bvar 0 with v. LHS: after opening, fvar x occurrences (those introduced) become v. But also original fvar x occurrences become v — so need x not free in t. Also, what about bvar 1 in t (bound by an outer binder)? Both sides leave it; fine.

Also, we need that substitution doesn't affect lc-ness... but maybe we don't need lc in LR. Values involve locally_closed though. Hmm.

Wait, maybe we can avoid the substitution function entirely by a different tactic: **interpret the context semantically by assigning LR terms to fvars and defining the fundamental theorem directly via a recursive "semantic typing" relation that avoids actual substitution**. That is, the standard "logical relation with an environment" where we don't substitute but instead prove a lemma "if Gamma |-- t \in T then for all rho ... LR T (subst rho t)". We can't avoid substitution since terms mention fvars.

Alternatively, we can define an *inductive* relation "t at type T in env Gamma" semantically — like a "semantic typing" judgment `semsyn Gamma t T` defined by recursion on t? Hmm.

OK here's another thought: since we only need SN for closed terms under `empty`, but as noted, terms typable under `empty` may contain bvars (inside abstractions). Hmm, wait, actually — the theorem is `forall t T, empty |-- t \in T -> SN t`. If t = tm_abs T (tm_bvar 0), SN holds (value). Could there be a typable term with a bvar in a redex position that loops? I doubt it: bvars only enter through T_Abs bodies, and opening replaces bvar 0; but consider `tm_abs A (tm_app (tm_bvar 0) (tm_bvar 0))` — typable? T_Abs requires open (app (bvar 0) (bvar 0)) (fvar x) = app (fvar x) (fvar x) typed under x : A. That requires A = A→B and x:A, x:B... so A = A→B impossible for finite types (occurs check) — unless... A = Bool? x: Bool can't be applied. So not typable. But `tm_abs A (tm_app (tm_bvar 0) (tm_bvar 1))`? Opening index 0 gives app (fvar x) (bvar 0) — bvar 0 free in body, cannot be typed (no rule types bvar). So indeed, all typable terms under empty are "essentially closed". But we don't need that.

Let's think about what's needed: the fundamental theorem in a form that handles open terms, since the T_Abs case goes under binders.

I think the cleanest formulation that avoids heavy subst infrastructure: define a **semantic typing relation that directly interprets terms under an atom→term environment, using a "closing substitution" function**, and prove the subst/open commutation lemma. It's a known pain but doable. Alternatively, use **a "closure" operation that replaces all fvars by their sigma images and also closes bvars with a special dummy**? Hmm.

Wait, maybe there's a much simpler way given the specific LR: Since LR T t implies SN t, and we want SN of the original term, perhaps we can state the fundamental theorem directly on the *open* terms with fvars interpreted by *values in LR*. The context interpretation in locally nameless form:

`sem_ctx Gamma sigma` where `sigma : atom -> tm` and `sem_ctx` says: for all x T, Gamma x = Some T -> LR T (sigma x).

Fundamental theorem: `Gamma |-- t \in T -> forall sigma, sem_ctx Gamma sigma -> LR T (subst sigma t)`.

Let me check the T_Abs case carefully with the commutation lemma:
- Given: `forall x, ~ In x L -> (x |-> T1 ; Gamma) |-- open t1 (fvar x) \in T2`.
- Want: LR (T1→T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)).
- By LR_abs: need for all values v with LR T1 v: LR T2 (open (subst sigma t1) v).
- Pick x fresh: x ∉ L, x not free in t1, x not in the "support" of sigma? We need `subst sigma (fvar x)` etc. Let's define sigma' := fun y => if eqb x y then v else sigma y. Then show `LR T2 (subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)))` from IH with x fresh, then rewrite via commutation lemma `subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) v` requiring x ∉ fvars(t1). Since sigma' agrees with sigma except at x: for y ≠ x, sigma' y = sigma y. In `subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))`: occurrences of fvar x in `open t1 (fvar x)` come from (a) bvar 0 opened, and (b) original fvar x in t1. If x ∉ fvars t1, only (a), replaced by sigma' x = v. On the RHS, `open (subst sigma t1) v` replaces bvar 0 with v. Both are equal by induction on t1 with proper handling of bvars: careful: when we descend under binders, `open t (fvar x)` at the top level only opens index 0; inside an abstraction, indices shift. Opening commutes with substitution as long as we handle indices. Specifically `subst sigma (open_rec k (fvar x) t) = open_rec k (subst sigma (fvar x)) (subst sigma t)`? Is that true? Let's check: subst replaces fvars, open_rec replaces bvar k. They act on disjoint constructors, so they commute: `subst sigma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t)`. Yes! Since subst only touches tm_fvar and open_rec only touches tm_bvar, they commute trivially. 

So `subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma' (fvar x)) (subst sigma' t1) = open v (subst sigma' t1)`. And `subst sigma' t1 = subst sigma t1` provided x ∉ fvars t1 and sigma' = sigma off x, plus... careful: `subst sigma' t1` replaces fvars by sigma'; since x ∉ fvars t1, it equals `subst sigma t1`. ✓. So no need for a special subst_open lemma — just the commutation of subst with open_rec and the pointwise equality. 

We need a freshness/choice of x: from T_Abs we get a cofinite L; we need x ∉ L and x ∉ fvars(t1). We can define `fvars : tm -> list atom` (collecting fvars) and prove a "exists fresh atom" lemma: there's an atom not in a given list. Since atoms are nat, easy: use a bound like `max of list + 1` or `length L`... We can prove: exists x, ~ In x L by taking e.g. `fold_right max 0 L + 1`, or S (list_max L)... Simplest: prove by induction on L that there exists a nat not in L (e.g., using `list_max` or a simple recursive function). Alternatively: `In x L -> x < S (list_max L)`?? Hmm, easier: define `fresh (L : list atom) := fold_right Nat.max 0 L`. Prove `~ In (S (fold_right Nat.max 0 L)) L` by induction on L. Since max is in Nat. Let's just prove: for all L, exists x, ~ In x L. By induction on L: [] → 0. (a::L) → if In a ... hmm. Easier: define upper bound. Let me do:

```
Lemma in_le_max : forall L x, In x L -> x <= fold_right Nat.max 0 L.
Lemma fresh_exists : forall L, exists x, ~ In x L.
Proof. intros L. exists (S (fold_right Nat.max 0 L)). intros H. apply in_le_max in H. lia. Qed.
```
Requires `lia` (from Stdlib Require Import Lia). We can import more stdlib modules (allowed: only supplied task and locally installed Rocq libraries). Yes, we can Require Import Lia, List, PeanoNat etc.

Even simpler: use `list_max`? Let's just do fold_right Nat.max.

Also need `fvars : tm -> list atom` and the freshness for the abstraction case. Alternatively, since the T_Abs hypothesis holds for *all* x not in L, and we can pick any x fresh from L ∪ fvars(t1) ∪ (other stuff), we need the finiteness of t1's fvars.

Now, we also need to handle the semantics of `subst sigma t` for the T_Abs case in terms of what the IH gives. But the IH is instantiated with the extended sigma'. We need `sem_ctx (update Gamma x T1) sigma'`: for all y T, update Gamma x T1 y = Some T -> LR T (sigma' y). Given sem_ctx Gamma sigma: 
- If y = x: update gives Some T1 (since Nat.eqb x x = true), so need LR T1 (sigma' x) = LR T1 v. ✓ (v is the value with LR T1 v).
- If y ≠ x: update gives Gamma y, so LR T (sigma y) = LR T (sigma' y) since sigma' y = sigma y. ✓.
Fine.

Now the other main work: the LR lemmas. Let's enumerate:

Definitions:
```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => strongly_normalizing t /\ (forall v, value v -> LR T1 v -> LR T2 (tm_app t v))
  end.
```

Lemmas:
(L1) `LR_SN : forall T t, LR T t -> SN t`. By induction on T; Bool trivial, arrow first component.
(L2) `SN_step : forall t t', SN t -> t --> t' -> SN t'.` By inversion on SN t: SN_intro gives forall t', t --> t' -> SN t'. ✓
(L3) `value_SN : forall v, value v -> SN v`. Also `value_lc : value v -> locally_closed v`.
(L4) `LR_step : forall T t t', LR T t -> t --> t' -> LR T t'.` By induction on T:
   - Bool: SN step. ✓
   - Arrow: t' SN from t SN. And for all values v with LR T1 v: LR T2 (t' v). We know LR T2 (t v) from arrow component. And t v --> t' v by ST_App1 with locally_closed v (from value_lc). Then IH(T2): LR T2 (t v) -> t v --> t' v -> LR T2 (t' v). ✓
   Note: this is structural induction on T, but LR is defined by recursion on T, so induction on T works. Actually careful: In the arrow case, IH for T1 and T2 available. Good.

(L5) `SN_app`? We might need the general "application of SN terms" — but as shown, that's false in general? Let's double check: SN t1 and SN t2 doesn't imply SN (t1 t2), since t1 = λx.xx (SN), t2 = λx.xx (SN), t1 t2 loops. Right, false. So we need typed/value info.

The application lemma (L6): `LR_app : forall A B t1 t2, LR (Ty_Arrow A B) t1 -> LR A t2 -> LR B (tm_app t1 t2)`.

Proof plan: We have H1 : LR (A→B) t1 = SN t1 ∧ (∀v, value v → LR A v → LR B (t1 v)); H2 : LR A t2 (hence SN t2 by L1).

Approach: induction on the SN derivation of t1 (H1.1), generalizing t2? But t2's type A is fixed; generalizing over t2 with LR A t2 works. But in the case t2 steps, we need induction on t2 too. Let's do: revert t2; induction on SN t1; in each case, intros t2 H2; then we need LR B (t1 t2). 

Case analysis on whether t1 is a value:
- If t1 is a value: then by H1.2 with v=t1 (value), need LR B (t1 t2) — but H1.2 gives LR B (t1 v) only for v with LR A v. t2 is not necessarily a value, only LR A t2. Hmm! So we need: if t1 is a value in LR (A→B) and LR A t2, then LR B (t1 t2). 

  Sub-case: t2 is a value: then LR B (t1 t2) by H1.2 directly. ✓
  Sub-case: t2 not a value: then t1 t2 --> t1 t2' for t2 --> t2' (ST_App2 requires value v1 = t1 ✓ and t2 --> t2'). By IH on SN t2 (nested induction), LR B (t1 t2') for all reducts t2'. Then need LR B (t1 t2) from "all reducts (one-step) are LR B". This is the closure lemma! Since t1 t2 is not a value (it's an application), we need: "if all one-step reducts of s are in LR B, then LR B s". But as discussed, this closure lemma requires the term s to not be an abstraction... Actually for the closure lemma applied to s = t1 t2 (an application), let's re-examine the arrow case: B = B1→B2. We need for all values v with LR B1 v: LR B2 ((t1 t2) v). Now (t1 t2) v is an application of an application. Its reducts: 
   - ST_App1 on the outer: (t1 t2) --> w gives w v. We know LR B (t1 t2)... circular (we're proving it).
   
  Hmm, the closure lemma needs care. Let's think about proving closure lemma by induction on B *and* using the hypothesis "all reducts in LR B":
  
  `LR_of_reducts : forall T t, (forall t', t --> t' -> LR T t') -> LR T t`? 
  
  Arrow case: need SN t: for each t' with t --> t', LR T t' gives SN t', so SN t by SN_intro ✓.
  Need for all values v with LR T1 v: LR T2 (t v). By IH on T2 (which is a subterm of T), it suffices to show all one-step reducts of (t v) are in LR T2. Reducts of t v:
   - ST_App1: t --> t', gives t' v. Apply H t' Hstep : LR (T1→T2) t', then arrow component with LR T1 v gives LR T2 (t' v) ✓.
   - ST_App2: v --> v'. But v is a value; values don't step. Need lemma `value_no_step`.
   - ST_AppAbs: t = tm_abs T' e with lc, v value: gives open e v. But this case is NOT covered by the hypothesis H. So the closure lemma FAILS for t an abstraction — as we knew.

  But for our use, t = t1 t2 (an application), so t = tm_app _ _ which is never of the form tm_abs. Is ST_AppAbs applicable to t v where t = tm_app ...? ST_AppAbs requires t = tm_abs T' e; t is an app, so no. So the closure lemma is fine for terms that are applications (or ifs) — but when we get to (t1 t2) v, that's also an application (of an app). So a closure lemma with hypothesis "t is not an abstraction" (or more simply "t is an app or if, i.e., not a value"? careful: t could be a non-value non-abstraction like tm_bvar) would work. Let's define:

  ```
  Lemma LR_of_reducts : forall T t,
     (forall t', t --> t' -> LR T t') ->
     (<something about t not being an abstraction>) ->
     LR T t.
  ```
  What exactly do we need? In the arrow case we need to handle reducts of (t v) where t is not an abstraction: ST_AppAbs can only fire when t is an abstraction. So condition: "t is not of the form tm_abs T e" — i.e., `forall T' e, t <> tm_abs T' e`. And (t v) is also an application, so it's not an abstraction. ✓. So define predicate:
  ```
  Definition not_abs (t : tm) : Prop := forall T e, t <> tm_abs T e.
  ```
  Then `LR_of_reducts : forall T t, not_abs t -> (forall t', t --> t' -> LR T t') -> LR T t.`
  
  Hmm wait, in the arrow case, we need `not_abs (tm_app t v)` which holds trivially. And in the Bool case (T = Ty_Bool), no extra condition needed: SN t from reducts. So induction on T works with the not_abs condition unused in base case.

  Actually hold on: in the arrow case, to apply IH at T2 to the term `tm_app t v`, we need `not_abs (tm_app t v)`. This is trivially provable. ✓.

  Also, do we need "all reducts are in LR T" for t v? The reducts are (a) t' v from ST_App1 — handled; (b) t v' from ST_App2 — impossible since v is a value; (c) open e v — impossible since not_abs t. ✓ 

  Great, so LR_of_reducts works with not_abs.

- Back to LR_app. Let's restructure:

Let me restructure the proof of LR_app more carefully. Since LR B (t1 t2) is what we want, and both t1 and t2 can step, the standard "Tait" proof:

Claim: for all t1 (SN), for all t2, LR (A→B) t1 -> LR A t2 -> LR B (t1 t2).

Proof by induction on SN t1:
  Given t1 with all reducts SN (Hreds) and IH: forall t1', t1 --> t1' -> LR (A→B) t1' -> forall t2, LR A t2 -> LR B (t1' t2).

  Hmm, actually including LR (A→B) t1' in the IH statement is good since we derive it from LR (A→B) t1 and L4 anyway.

  Then to show LR B (t1 t2) for LR A t2, use LR_of_reducts at B on the term t1 t2 (not_abs ✓):
    For each reduct:
    - t1' t2 (App1): need LR B (t1' t2): IH with LR (A→B) t1' = L4 applied ✓, and t2.
    - t1 t2' (App2, so t1 is a value): need LR B (t1 t2'). Now t2' is SN and LR A t2'. We need to close under t2 steps. Hmm, we could use LR_of_reducts at B on t1 t2'... but that's what we need. Circular unless we do a nested induction on SN t2.
    - open e t2 (AppAbs, t1 = tm_abs T e, t2 value): need LR B (open e t2). This requires knowing the abstraction's body semantics.       Hmm, actually we need `SN t1` and the arrow property separately; but LR (A→B) t1 gives SN t1, so we can induct on that.
      
      Maybe simplest: do a **double induction**: induction on SN t1, and within the proof of LR B (t1 t2), again destruct whether t1 is a value / t2 is a value, using an inner induction on SN t2 for the case where t1 is a value and t2 steps.

      The cleanest in Rocq: prove two lemmas:
      (i) `LR_app_value : forall A B v1, value v1 -> LR (A→B) v1 -> forall t2, LR A t2 -> LR B (tm_app v1 t2).`

            - App2: value t1, t2 --> t2', reduct t1 t2': apply lemma (i) with v1 = t1, value, LR (A→B) t1, t2' (LR A t2' from L4) ✓. Wait, but I should double check LR_of_reducts itself is provable by induction on T, since the arrow case requires applying it at T2 (a subterm) to the term `tm_app t v`. Let's write:

```
Lemma LR_of_reducts : forall T t,
  not_abs t ->
  (forall t', t --> t' -> LR T t') ->
  LR T t.
Proof.
  induction T as [| T1 IH1 T2 IH2]; intros t Hna Hreds.
  - (* Bool *) apply SN_intro. intros t' Hstep. apply Hreds in Hstep. destruct Hstep... 
    (* LR Ty_Bool t' = SN t' *) assumption.
  - (* Arrow *) split.
    + apply SN_intro. intros t' Hstep. apply Hreds in Hstep. apply Hstep. (* LR (T1→T2) t' = SN t' ∧ ... *) 
    + intros v Hv HLR1. apply IH2 with (t := tm_app t v).
      * intros T' e Heq. discriminate Heq.  (* not_abs (tm_app t v) *)
      * intros w Hstep. inversion Hstep; subst.
        -- (* ST_AppAbs: t = tm_abs T' t0 *) exfalso; apply Hna; reflexivity... 
           Hmm, ST_AppAbs gives t = tm_abs T t0, contradicting Hna.
        -- (* ST_App1 *) apply Hreds in H. destruct H as [? ?]. apply H1. assumption.
           (* LR (T1→T2) t1' gives the arrow component *)
        -- (* ST_App2: v --> v' *) exfalso; apply value_no_step with v; assumption.
  Qed.
```
Wait, in the arrow case, the IH is `IH1 : forall t, not_abs t -> (forall t', t --> t' -> LR T1 t') -> LR T1 t` and `IH2 : ... LR T2 ...`. Now the fundamental theorem. Let's define substitution:

```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2) (subst sigma t3)
  end.
```

And `subst_open_rec : forall k u sigma t, subst sigma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t)`. Prove by induction on t. For tm_bvar i: if i = k then u else bvar i. LHS: subst sigma (if eqb k i then u else bvar i). RHS: if eqb k i then subst sigma u else subst sigma (bvar i) = if ... then subst sigma u else bvar i. Need: subst sigma (if b then u else bvar i) = if b then subst sigma u else bvar i — by destruct b. Fine.
For tm_abs: LHS subst sigma (abs T (open_rec (S k) u t1)) = abs T (subst sigma (open_rec (S k) u t1)) = abs T (open_rec (S k) (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t1)) by IH. RHS = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (abs T (subst sigma t1)) = abs T (open_rec (S k) (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t1)). ✓. Note: this works because our subst doesn't touch bvars at all. 

`open` = open_rec 0, so `subst_open : subst sigma (open u t) = open (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t)`.

Fundamental theorem:
```
Definition sem_ctx (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> LR T (sigma x).

Lemma typing_LR : forall Gamma t T,
  Gamma |-- t \in T ->
  forall sigma, sem_ctx Gamma sigma -> LR T (subst sigma t).
```
Proof by induction on the typing derivation.

- T_Var: Gamma x = Some T; subst sigma (fvar x) = sigma x; apply Hsem. ✓
- T_Abs: Gamma |-- abs T1 t1 \in T1 -> T2, with Hbody : forall x, ~ In x L -> (update Gamma x T1) |-- open t1 (fvar x) \in T2.
  Goal: LR (T1→T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)).
  Apply LR_abs: need forall v, value v -> LR T1 v -> LR T2 (open (subst sigma t1) v).
  Take v, Hv, HLR.
  Pick x fresh from L ++ fvars t1 (and maybe we need x not to clash with anything else? sigma is arbitrary; we need subst sigma' t1 = subst sigma t1 where sigma' is sigma except x↦v. So need x ∉ fvars t1.)
  Let sigma' := fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else sigma y.
  Have Hbody_x := Hbody x Hfresh.
  IH (with the semantic context update Gamma x T1 and sigma'): gives LR T2 (subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))).
  Rewrite: subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma' (fvar x)) (subst sigma' t1) = open v (subst sigma' t1) = open v (subst sigma t1).
    - subst sigma' (fvar x) = sigma' x = v (eqb x x = true). ✓
    - subst sigma' t1 = subst sigma t1 since for y ∈ fvars t1, y ≠ x, so sigma' y = sigma y. Need a lemma: `(forall y, In y (fvars t1) -> sigma' y = sigma y) -> subst sigma' t1 = subst sigma t1`. Or simpler: since x ∉ fvars t1, `subst sigma' t1 = subst sigma t1`. Prove by induction on t1: `subst_ext : forall sigma1 sigma2 t, (forall y, In y (fvars t) -> sigma1 y = sigma2 y) -> subst sigma1 t = subst sigma2 t`. ✓
  So we need to prove sem_ctx (update Gamma x T1) sigma':
    - y with update Gamma x T1 y = Some T: if y = x then T = T1 and sigma' x = v: need LR T1 v ✓; else Gamma y = Some T, sem_ctx Gamma sigma gives LR T (sigma y) and sigma' y = sigma y (y ≠ x) ✓.
  
  Also need x fresh: x ∉ L and x ∉ fvars t1. Get from a fresh lemma: exists x, ~ In x (L ++ fvars t1).

- T_App: IH1: LR (T1→T2) (subst sigma t1); IH2: LR T1 (subst sigma t2). Apply LR_app. ✓
- T_True/T_False: LR Bool tm_true = SN tm_true. Prove SN_true, SN_false via SN_intro (no steps). ✓
- T_If: IH0: LR Bool (subst sigma t1) = SN (...); IH1: LR T (subst sigma t2); IH2: LR T (subst sigma t3). Apply LR_if. ✓

Then normalization: for empty |-- t \in T, take sigma = identity (fun x => tm_fvar x)? sem_ctx empty sigma holds vacuously (empty x = None, impossible). Gives LR T (subst id t) = LR T t. Then LR_SN gives SN t. ✓

Wait: `subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t` — prove by induction (identity subst). ✓

The typical issue: variables in the context that appear in the conclusion may need reverting. Since we did `intros Gamma t T Hty` and then `induction Hty`, the induction motive is `fun (Gamma : context) (t : tm) (T : ty) (H : has_type Gamma t T) => forall sigma, sem_ctx Gamma sigma -> LR T (subst sigma t)`, which is fine because sigma is introduced after. Coq will automatically generalize the conclusion over sigma if we do `induction Hty` right after intros... Actually `intros Gamma t T Hty. induction Hty.` — the goal is `forall sigma, sem_ctx Gamma sigma -> ...`. The induction tactic will revert dependent variables and try to abstract; it typically works with a conclusion of the form `forall sigma, ...` because it can keep sigma universally quantified in the motive. I think Coq handles this (it generalizes the goal over all variables not appearing in the inductive's parameters... hmm, it might complain "cannot find the elimination combinator" or produce IHs without the forall sigma). Safer: `intros Gamma t T Hty sigma Hsem. generalize dependent sigma.` Hmm, that would be wrong order. Let's do:

```
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T Hty.
  induction Hty; intros sigma Hsem.
```
I believe this works: after `intros ... Hty`, the goal is `forall sigma, sem_ctx Gamma sigma -> LR T (subst sigma t)`. `induction Hty` — Coq's induction will look at the goal; variables Gamma, t, T are in the context. Standard behavior: it reverts the goal's dependence and creates motive `fun Gamma t T H => forall sigma, ...`. Coq usually handles this well. Let's just try it.

Lemma LR_if : forall T t0 t1 t2, strongly_normalizing t0 -> LR T t1 -> LR T t2 -> LR T (tm_if t0 t1 t2).

    + (* ST_IfTrue *) assumption.  (* t' = t1, need LR T t1 ✓ *)
    + (* ST_IfFalse *) assumption.
    + (* ST_If *) apply IH; assumption.  (* IH : forall t0', t0 --> t0' -> forall t1 t2, LR T t1 -> LR T t2 -> LR T (if t0' t1 t2) *)

11. `fvars` function and freshness:
```
Fixpoint fvars (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fvars t1 ++ fvars t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fvars t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fvars t1 ++ fvars t2 ++ fvars t3
  end.
```

Freshness lemma: `exists x, ~ In x (L1 ++ L2)`. General: for any L, exists x, ~ In x L.

Proof via max:
```
Lemma exists_fresh : forall L : list atom, exists x, ~ In x L.
Proof.
  intros L. exists (S (fold_right Nat.max 0 L)).
  intros Hin. 
  assert (H : forall x, In x L -> x <= fold_right Nat.max 0 L).
  { induction L as [| a L IH]; simpl; intros x Hx.
    - destruct Hx.
    - destruct Hx as [-> | Hx].
      + apply Nat.le_max_l.  (* hmm, fold_right Nat.max 0 (a :: L) = Nat.max a (fold_right Nat.max 0 L) *)
      + apply Nat.le_trans with (fold_right Nat.max 0 L); [apply IH; assumption | apply Nat.le_max_r]. }
  apply H in Hin. lia.
Qed.
```
The `simpl` will unfold fold_right: fold_right Nat.max 0 (a::L) = Nat.max a (fold_right Nat.max 0 L). ✓. We could use `Nat.le_max_l`, `Nat.le_max_r` from PeanoNat (already imported). And `lia` needs `From Stdlib Require Import Lia`. Let's add that (allowed). Alternatively prove without lia: x <= fold_max -> x < S fold_max by Nat.lt_succ_r / le_n_S. `apply Nat.lt_succ_r`? We need `x < S M` from `x <= M`: `apply Nat.lt_succ_r; assumption` or `Nat.lt_le_trans`... `Nat.lt_succ_r : forall n m, n < S m <-> n <= m`. So `apply Nat.lt_succ_r; assumption`. We can avoid Lia entirely. But importing Lia is fine and handy. I'll import Lia to make things easier. Standard library is locally installed. ✓

Actually, alternatively, simpler freshness: since L is finite, use `list_max`? Let's just use the fold_right max approach.

Hmm, even simpler: use the fact that `In x L -> x <= length L + fold...`. Meh, fold is fine. Or use `NoDup`? Let's do the max approach.

Now the fundamental theorem T_Abs case detail. Let's write the sigma' extension and freshness.

```
Definition extend (sigma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (v : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else sigma y.
```

Need:
- `extend_eq : forall sigma x v, extend sigma x v x = v`. `unfold extend; rewrite Nat.eqb_refl; reflexivity.`
- `extend_neq : forall sigma x v y, x <> y -> extend sigma x v y = sigma y`. `unfold extend; rewrite Nat.eqb_neq by assumption; reflexivity.` Hmm, `Nat.eqb_neq : forall n m, n <> m -> (n =? m) = false`? Let me recall: `Nat.eqb_eq : forall n m, (n =? m) = true <-> n = m`; `Nat.eqb_neq : forall n m, (n =? m) = false <-> n <> m`. So `rewrite Nat.eqb_neq; assumption` or `apply Nat.eqb_neq; assumption`... Actually to rewrite `(x =? y) = false` we can do `apply Nat.eqb_neq; assumption` where the goal after unfold is `(if (x =? y) then v else sigma y) = sigma y`; `destruct (x =? y) eqn:E`; then `apply Nat.eqb_eq in E` → contradiction, or `apply Nat.eqb_neq in E`... Let's just use `Nat.eqb_neq` directly: `rewrite (Nat.eqb_neq x y H)`? The statement `Nat.eqb_neq : forall n m : nat, (n =? m) = false <-> n <> m`. So `rewrite (proj2 (Nat.eqb_neq x y) H)`? Hmm, proj2 with H : x <> y gives (x =? y) = false. Then `rewrite that; reflexivity`. OK.

Actually, `update` in the file uses `if Nat.eqb x y`. We'll need similar lemmas for update:
- `update_eq : forall Gamma x T, update Gamma x T x = Some T`.
- `update_neq : forall Gamma x y T, x <> y -> update Gamma x y T = Gamma y`. Careful with argument order: `update Gamma x T = fun y => if eqb x y then Some T else Gamma y`.

Now the sem_ctx for the extended context:
```
Lemma sem_ctx_extend : forall Gamma sigma x T1 v,
  sem_ctx Gamma sigma -> LR T1 v ->
  sem_ctx (update Gamma x T1) (extend sigma x v).
Proof.
  intros Gamma sigma x T1 v Hsem HLR y T Hy.
  unfold update in Hy.
  destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
  - apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst y. simpl... 
    (* Hy : Some T1 = Some T *) injection Hy as ->. 
    unfold extend. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. apply HLR.
  - (* Hy : Gamma y = Some T *) 
    unfold extend. rewrite E. apply Hsem. assumption.
Qed.
```
Careful: after `destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E`, in the true branch Hy : Some T1 = Some T. So T = T1. `injection Hy as HT; subst T`. Then goal `LR T1 (extend sigma x v y)` = `LR T1 (if (x =? y) then v else sigma y)`; with E : (x =? y) = true, rewrite E, apply HLR ✓.
False branch: Hy : Gamma y = Some T; goal `LR T (extend sigma x v y)` = `LR T (if (x =? y) then v else sigma y)`; rewrite E; apply Hsem; assumption ✓.

Now freshness and the T_Abs case:

```
  - (* T_Abs *) 
    (* H : forall x, ~ In x L -> update Gamma x T1 |-- open t1 (fvar x) \in T2 *)
    (* IH : forall x, ~ In x L -> forall sigma, sem_ctx (update Gamma x T1) sigma -> LR T2 (subst sigma (open t1 (fvar x))) *)
    intros sigma Hsem.
    apply LR_abs.
    intros v Hv HLR1.
    (* pick fresh x *)
    destruct (exists_fresh (L ++ fvars t1)) as [x Hx].
    apply not_in_app in Hx; destruct Hx as [HxL HxFV].
    specialize (IH x HxL (extend sigma x v)).
    specialize (IH (sem_ctx_extend Gamma sigma x T1 v Hsem HLR1)).
    (* IH : LR T2 (subst (extend sigma x v) (open t1 (fvar x))) *)
    rewrite subst_open_rec in IH.  (* subst sigma (open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1) = open_rec 0 (subst sigma (fvar x)) (subst sigma t1) *)
```
Hmm, careful: `subst (extend sigma x v) (open t1 (tm_fvar x))` — `open t1 (fvar x)` = `open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1`. Using subst_open_rec with k=0, u = fvar x, sigma = extend sigma x v, t = t1: gives `open_rec 0 (subst (extend sigma x v) (fvar x)) (subst (extend sigma x v) t1)`. Then `subst (extend sigma x v) (fvar x) = extend sigma x v x = v`. Then `subst (extend sigma x v) t1 = subst sigma t1` by subst_ext with the freshness x ∉ fvars t1.

So: 
```
    rewrite subst_open_rec in IH.
    rewrite extend_eq in IH.  (* subst ... (fvar x) = v *)
    rewrite (subst_ext_fresh ...) in IH.  (* subst (extend sigma x v) t1 = subst sigma t1, given x ∉ fvars t1 *)
    exact IH.
```
where `subst_ext_fresh : forall sigma1 sigma2 t x, ~ In x (fvars t) -> (forall y, sigma1 y = sigma2 y \/ (y = x ...))` hmm. Let me define:
```
Lemma subst_ext : forall (sigma1 sigma2 : atom -> tm) t,
  (forall x, In x (fvars t) -> sigma1 x = sigma2 x) ->
  subst sigma1 t = subst sigma2 t.
```
And to use it: need forall y, In y (fvars t1) -> extend sigma x v y = sigma y, which follows from y ≠ x (since x ∉ fvars t1 and In y (fvars t1) implies y ≠ x — need to show that: if y = x then x ∈ fvars t1, contradiction). Fine:
```
    erewrite subst_ext with (sigma1 := extend sigma x v) (sigma2 := sigma).
    - reflexivity... 
```
Better formulate as a direct lemma:
```
Lemma subst_extend_neq : forall sigma x v t,
  ~ In x (fvars t) -> subst (extend sigma x v) t = subst sigma t.
Proof.
  intros sigma x v t Hx. apply subst_ext. intros y Hy.
  apply extend_neq. intros Heq. subst y. apply Hx; assumption.
Qed.
```
Then the T_Abs case: `rewrite subst_extend_neq in IH by assumption.` Hmm, rewriting in a hypothesis with a side condition: `rewrite (subst_extend_neq sigma x v t1 HxFV) in IH.`

Wait, but there's a subtlety: `subst (extend sigma x v) (open t1 (fvar x))` — inside this, the fvar x occurs both from opening and possibly from t1. We're rewriting the inner part `subst ... t1`? No — the rewrite `subst_open_rec` turns it into an `open_rec` with `subst (extend...) (fvar x)` and `subst (extend...) t1`. Then the second is rewritten by subst_extend_neq. ✓ The occurrence of fvar x in `open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1` doesn't matter after the substitution is factored. ✓

Great, so the T_Abs case works.

Wait, one more check: `LR_abs` statement. Let me define:
```
Lemma LR_abs : forall A B e,
  (forall v, value v -> LR A v -> LR B (open e v)) ->
  LR (Ty_Arrow A B) (tm_abs A e).

Note: `open e v = open_rec 0 v e`. In the arrow component we need `LR B (tm_app (tm_abs A e) v)`, and the reduct `open e v` matches ST_AppAbs's result `open t v` with t = e ✓.

Now, hmm, one important check: in LR_abs's proof, when analyzing steps of `tm_app (tm_abs A e) v`, the ST_AppAbs case requires the equation `tm_abs A e = tm_abs T t`, giving T = A, t = e. Fine.

Let's now also handle: does `LR_abs` need `locally_closed` for the SN component? SN of tm_abs A e: no steps ✓.

Now let me also double check the fundamental theorem's T_App case: IH1 gives LR (T1→T2) (subst sigma t1); IH2 gives LR T1 (subst sigma t2). Goal: LR T2 (subst sigma (tm_app t1 t2)) = LR T2 (tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)). Apply LR_app ✓.

T_If case: IH0: LR Bool (subst sigma t1) = SN (subst sigma t1) — hmm, LR Ty_Bool t is defined as `strongly_normalizing t`, so IH0 : LR Bool (subst sigma t1) reduces to SN (subst sigma t1) after simpl. T_True: goal LR Bool (subst sigma tm_true) = LR Bool tm_true = SN tm_true ✓.

Now, the final theorem: 
```
Theorem normalization : forall t T, empty |-- t \in T -> SN t.
Proof.
  intros t T Hty.
  pose proof (typing_LR empty t T Hty (fun x => tm_fvar x)) as H.
  - (* sem_ctx empty (fun x => fvar x) *) intros x T' Hx. unfold empty in Hx. discriminate.
  - rewrite subst_identity in H. apply LR_SN in H. exact H.
Qed.
```

Now let's write the full development.

```coq
(** ** Auxiliary definitions for the strong normalization proof *)

Definition subst_tm (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm sigma t1) (subst_tm sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_tm sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst_tm sigma t1) (subst_tm sigma t2) (subst_tm sigma t3)
  end.
```
Wait, this is not a Fixpoint (no recursive call syntax issue: it is structurally recursive on t, so `Fixpoint` works; using `Definition` with match won't allow recursive calls). Use Fixpoint.

```
Fixpoint subst_tm (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm sigma t1) (subst_tm sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_tm sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 =>
      tm_if (subst_tm sigma t1) (subst_tm sigma t2) (subst_tm sigma t3)
  end.
```

```
Fixpoint fvars (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fvars t1 ++ fvars t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fvars t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fvars t1 ++ fvars t2 ++ fvars t3
  end.
```

```
Definition extend (sigma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (v : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else sigma y.

Definition not_abs (t : tm) : Prop := forall T e, t <> tm_abs T e.

Definition sem_ctx (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> LR T (sigma x).
```
(preceded by LR definition)

LR must be defined before sem_ctx but after strongly_normalizing.

```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall v, value v -> LR T1 v -> LR T2 (tm_app t v))
  end.
```

```coq
Lemma value_no_step : forall v t', value v -> v --> t' -> False.
Proof.
  intros v t' Hv Hstep. induction Hv; inversion Hstep.
Qed.
```
Hmm, `induction Hv` will generalize v? Hv : value v with v a variable from intros; induction Hv on the value derivation with the goal `False` and Hstep depending on v... Actually Hstep : v --> t' depends on v. `induction Hv` will revert Hstep? The typical pattern: `intros v t' Hv Hstep. induction Hv.` — Coq will complain that Hstep depends on v? Since v is a variable introduced by intros and Hv : value v, `induction Hv` generalizes v in the goal but Hstep mentions v; Coq automatically reverts Hstep (it's a dependent hypothesis) — actually the tactic will fail with "Cannot generalize v because Hstep depends on it"? Hmm, `induction Hv` where Hstep : v --> t' — I think Coq reverts Hstep automatically if needed. Let me instead do:
```
Proof.
  intros v t' Hv. revert t'. induction Hv; intros t' Hstep; inversion Hstep.
Qed.
```
Here `induction Hv` happens before t' and Hstep are introduced, so no dependency issue. ✓ But `revert t'` — at that point t' is introduced; goal `value v -> v --> t' -> False`; we intro Hv then revert t'. Structure: `intros v t' Hv. revert t'. induction Hv; intros t' Hstep; inversion Hstep.` ✓

Then in each case (v_abs, v_true, v_false), goal is False with Hstep : (tm_abs T t) --> t'. `inversion Hstep` should solve.

Hmm, will `inversion Hstep` close a goal of False? Inversion on an impossible hypothesis closes the goal ✓.

```coq

  intros v Hv. induction Hv.
  - assumption.
  - apply lc_true.
  - apply lc_false.
Qed.

`induction Hv` gives goal locally_closed (tm_abs T t) with hypothesis locally_closed (tm_abs T t) — assumption ✓. For v_true: locally_closed tm_true = lc_at 0 tm_true, apply lc_true ✓.

```coq

The constructor SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', t --> t' -> SN t') -> SN t. Destructing HSN : SN t should give Hreds : forall t', t --> t' -> SN t' (with t fixed). ✓

```coq
Lemma LR_SN : forall T t, LR T t -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  induction T as [| T1 IH1 T2 IH2]; intros t HLR; simpl in HLR.
  - exact HLR.
  - destruct HLR as [HSN _]. exact HSN.
Qed.
```

```coq
Lemma LR_step : forall T t t', LR T t -> t --> t' -> LR T t'.
Proof.
  induction T as [| T1 IH1 T2 IH2]; intros t t' HLR Hstep; simpl in *.

Qed.

```coq
Lemma LR_of_reducts : forall T t,
  not_abs t ->
  (forall t', t --> t' -> LR T t') ->
  LR T t.

    + apply SN_intro. intros t' Hstep. destruct (Hreds t' Hstep) as [HSN _]. exact HSN.
    + intros v Hv HLRv. apply IH2 with (t := tm_app t v).

```coq
Lemma LR_if : forall T t0 t1 t2,
  strongly_normalizing t0 -> LR T t1 -> LR T t2 -> LR T (tm_if t0 t1 t2).
Proof.
  intros T t0 t1 t2 HSN0. revert t1 t2. 
  induction HSN0 as [t0 Hreds IH].
  intros t1 t2 H1 H2.
  apply LR_of_reducts with (t := tm_if t0 t1 t2).
  - intros T' e' Heq. discriminate Heq.
  - intros w Hstep. inversion Hstep; subst.
    + assumption.   (* ST_IfTrue: w = t1, need LR T t1 = H1 *)
    + assumption.   (* ST_IfFalse: w = t2 = H2 *)
    + apply IH; assumption.  (* ST_If: t0 --> t1', IH : forall t0', t0 --> t0' -> forall t1 t2, LR T t1 -> LR T t2 -> LR T (if t0' t1 t2) *)
Qed.

Now the substitution lemmas. Then:

```coq
Lemma subst_open_rec : forall sigma u t k,
  subst_tm sigma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst_tm sigma u) (subst_tm sigma t).
Proof.
  intros sigma u t. induction t as [i | x | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 | T t1 IH | | | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 t3 IH3]; intros k; simpl.
  - destruct (Nat.eqb k i); reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH1, IH2. reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH. reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH1, IH2, IH3. reflexivity.
Qed.
```
Careful with the abs case: goal after simpl: `tm_abs T (subst_tm sigma (open_rec (S k) u t1)) = tm_abs T (open_rec (S k) (subst_tm sigma u) (subst_tm sigma t1))`. `rewrite IH` where IH : forall k, ... — `rewrite IH` will instantiate k with (S k) ✓ and rewrite the LHS ✓. Then reflexivity ✓. Maybe need `f_equal; apply IH` since rewrite might rewrite in the wrong place. `rewrite IH` should match the LHS subterm exactly ✓.

For the app case: goal `tm_app (subst_tm sigma (open_rec k u t1)) (subst_tm sigma (open_rec k u t2)) = tm_app (open_rec k (subst_tm sigma u) (subst_tm sigma t1)) (open_rec k (subst_tm sigma u) (subst_tm sigma t2))`; rewrite IH1, IH2 ✓.

For bvar: as discussed. But careful: `simpl` on `subst_tm sigma (open_rec k u (tm_bvar i))`: open_rec k u (tm_bvar i) = if k =? i then u else tm_bvar i; then subst_tm sigma (if ...) — will `simpl` reduce the match on the if? `simpl` may not destruct the `if` automatically, leaving `subst_tm sigma (if Nat.eqb k i then u else tm_bvar i)`. Hmm, `simpl` might not push into the match because subst_tm is a Fixpoint matching on its argument; when the argument is a `if`, simpl could reduce to a match on the if... it typically leaves it as is. Using `destruct (Nat.eqb k i); reflexivity` should work: after destruct, the argument becomes u or tm_bvar i, and `simpl` will reduce. But the goal RHS also has the if; after destruct both sides reduce. `destruct ... ; reflexivity` might not simplify automatically; use `destruct (Nat.eqb k i); simpl; reflexivity`. Let me write `destruct (Nat.eqb k i) eqn:E; simpl; reflexivity.` Hmm, if simplification doesn't fire, we can add `cbn [subst_tm open_rec]`. We'll iterate.

Now `subst_extend_neq`, fvars helpers, exists_fresh, sem_ctx_extend, subst_identity, typing_LR.

```coq
Lemma not_in_app_l : forall (l1 l2 : list atom) x, ~ In x (l1 ++ l2) -> ~ In x l1.
Proof. intros l1 l2 x H Hin. apply H. apply in_or_app. left. assumption. Qed.
Lemma not_in_app_r : forall (l1 l2 : list atom) x, ~ In x (l1 ++ l2) -> ~ In x l2.
Proof. intros l1 l2 x H Hin. apply H. apply in_or_app. right. assumption. Qed.
```

```coq
Lemma subst_extend_neq : forall sigma x v t,
  ~ In x (fvars t) -> subst_tm (extend sigma x v) t = subst_tm sigma t.
Proof.
  intros sigma x v t. induction t as [i | y | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 | T t1 IH | | | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 t3 IH3]; intros Hx; simpl.
  - reflexivity.
  - unfold extend. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst y. exfalso. apply Hx. simpl. left. reflexivity.
    + reflexivity.
  - f_equal.
    + apply IH1. apply not_in_app_l in Hx. assumption.
    + apply IH2. apply not_in_app_r in Hx. assumption.
  - f_equal. apply IH. assumption.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - f_equal.
    + apply IH1. apply not_in_app_l in Hx. assumption.
    + f_equal.
      * apply IH2. apply not_in_app_l in Hx. apply not_in_app_r in Hx. assumption.
      * apply IH3. apply not_in_app_r in Hx. apply not_in_app_r in Hx. assumption.
Qed.
```
Hmm, careful with `apply not_in_app_l in Hx` when Hx is used later — `apply ... in Hx` will transform Hx; if I need Hx twice (for t2 and t3), the second `apply not_in_app_r in Hx` after the first modified it — I used a fresh variable? In the if case: Hx : ~ In x (fvars t1 ++ fvars t2 ++ fvars t3), which parses as fvars t1 ++ (fvars t2 ++ fvars t3). For t2: apply not_in_app_r to get Hx' : ~ In x (fvars t2 ++ fvars t3), then not_in_app_l. Since the f_equal subgoals are separate, Hx isn't destructively modified across goals? `apply ... in Hx` modifies the hypothesis in the current goal only, and each subgoal after f_equal has its own copy ✓. But in the same subgoal, if I do `apply not_in_app_l in Hx` then `apply not_in_app_r in Hx`, the second applies to the modified Hx — careful to do the right order. For t2's subgoal: first r then l. Lemma in_le_fold_max : forall (L : list atom) x, In x L -> x <= fold_right Nat.max 0 L.
Proof.
  intros L. induction L as [| a L IH]; intros x Hx.
  - destruct Hx.
  - simpl in *. destruct Hx as [-> | Hx].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (fold_right Nat.max 0 L); [apply IH; assumption | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh : forall L : list atom, exists x, ~ In x L.
Proof.

  exact (Nat.nle_succ_diag_l _ Hx).
Qed.

```coq
Theorem normalization : forall t T,
  <{ empty |-- t \in T }> ->
  strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  intros t T Hty.
  pose proof (typing_LR empty t T Hty (fun x => tm_fvar x)) as H.
  assert (Hsem : sem_ctx empty (fun x => tm_fvar x)).
  { intros x T' Hx. unfold empty in Hx. discriminate Hx. }
  specialize (H Hsem).
  rewrite subst_identity in H.
  apply LR_SN in H.
  exact H.
Qed.
```

```coq
(** ** Strong normalization proof *)

(** Term substitution for free variables. *)
Fixpoint subst_tm (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm sigma t1) (subst_tm sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_tm sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 =>
      tm_if (subst_tm sigma t1) (subst_tm sigma t2) (subst_tm sigma t3)
  end.

(** Free variables of a term. *)
Fixpoint fvars (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fvars t1 ++ fvars t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fvars t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fvars t1 ++ fvars t2 ++ fvars t3
  end.

(** Environment extension. *)
Definition extend (sigma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (v : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else sigma y.

(** A term that is not an abstraction. *)
Definition not_abs (t : tm) : Prop := forall T e, t <> tm_abs T e.

(** Unary, type-indexed logical relation. *)
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall v, value v -> LR T1 v -> LR T2 (tm_app t v))
  end.

(** Interpretation of typing contexts by semantic substitution. *)
Definition sem_ctx (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> LR T (sigma x).
```

Then lemmas. Let me write them all out and then compile.

```coq
Theorem normalization : forall t T,
  <{ empty |-- t \in T }> ->
  strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  intros t T Hty.
  assert (Hsem : sem_ctx empty (fun x => tm_fvar x)).
  { intros x T0 Hx. unfold empty in Hx. discriminate Hx. }
  pose proof (typing_LR empty t T Hty (fun x => tm_fvar x) Hsem) as H.
  rewrite subst_identity in H.
  apply LR_SN in H.
  exact H.
Qed.

Draft of the full replacement:

```coq
Inductive strongly_normalizing : tm -> Prop :=
  | SN_intro : forall t,
      (forall t', t --> t' -> strongly_normalizing t') ->
      strongly_normalizing t.

(** ** Logical relation for call-by-value strong normalization *)

(** Free-variable substitution. *)
Fixpoint subst_tm (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm sigma t1) (subst_tm sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_tm sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 =>
      tm_if (subst_tm sigma t1) (subst_tm sigma t2) (subst_tm sigma t3)
  end.

(** Free variables of a term. *)
Fixpoint fvars (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fvars t1 ++ fvars t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fvars t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fvars t1 ++ fvars t2 ++ fvars t3
  end.

(** Extension of a semantic substitution. *)
Definition extend (sigma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (v : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else sigma y.

Definition is_abs (t : tm) : bool :=
  match t with
  | tm_abs _ _ => true
  | _ => false
  end.

Definition not_abs (t : tm) : Prop := is_abs t = false.

(** The unary, type-indexed logical relation. *)
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall v, value v -> LR T1 v -> LR T2 (tm_app t v))
  end.

(** Context interpretation: sigma maps each variable of the context
    to a term in the logical relation at its type. *)
Definition sem_ctx (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> LR T (sigma x).
```

```coq
(** ** Basic facts *)

Lemma value_no_step : forall v t', value v -> v --> t' -> False.
Proof.
  intros v t' Hv. revert t'.
  induction Hv; intros t' Hstep; inversion Hstep.
Qed.

Lemma value_lc : forall v, value v -> locally_closed v.
Proof.
  intros v Hv. induction Hv.
  - assumption.
  - apply lc_true.
  - apply lc_false.
Qed.

Lemma SN_step : forall t t',
  strongly_normalizing t -> t --> t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' HSN Hstep. destruct HSN as [t0 Hreds]. apply Hreds. assumption.
Qed.
```
Hmm: `destruct HSN as [t0 Hreds]` — the SN_intro constructor takes `t` as an explicit argument, so destructing HSN : SN t where t is a variable gives... the constructor is `SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', t --> t' -> SN t') -> SN t`. When destructing the hypothesis `HSN : strongly_normalizing t` with t a variable, Coq's destruct on an inductive with a "non-uniform parameter"? SN has no parameters, so the term t is an index/argument. destruct HSN will substitute t := t0 (the constructor's argument) and give Hreds : forall t', t0 --> t' -> SN t'. Since t is a variable, unification sets t0 := t, so Hreds : forall t', t --> t' -> SN t' ✓. Good.

But careful: `destruct HSN` might fail due to the "dependent" nature if other hypotheses mention t (like Hstep : t --> t').   intros t t' HSN. destruct HSN as [t0 Hreds]. intros Hstep. apply Hreds. assumption.
Qed.
```
Then Hstep isn't in context at destruct time ✓.

Lemma LR_app_value : forall A B (v1 : tm),
  value v1 ->
  LR (Ty_Arrow A B) v1 ->
  forall t2, LR A t2 -> LR B (tm_app v1 t2).

  simpl in HLR1. destruct HLR1 as [HSN1 Harr].
  assert (Haux : forall t2, strongly_normalizing t2 -> LR A t2 -> LR B (tm_app v1 t2)).

Alternative approach: prove helper lemmas that characterize steps:

```
Lemma step_app_inv : forall t1 t2 t',
  tm_app t1 t2 --> t' ->
  (exists T e v, t1 = tm_abs T e /\ t2 = v /\ value v /\ t' = open e v)
  \/ (exists t1', t1 --> t1' /\ t' = tm_app t1' t2)
  \/ (exists v1 t2', t1 = v1 /\ value v1 /\ t2 --> t2' /\ t' = tm_app v1 t2').
Proof. intros. inversion H; subst; eauto. Qed.
```
This is basically what inversion gives, but as an explicit disjunction I can destruct with clean naming. That might actually be cleaner to work with! Let me consider.

Why? `eapply value_no_step` creates goals `value ?v` and `?v --> ?t'`. eassumption for `value ?v`: hypotheses include `H1 : value t` and `Hv : value v`; Coq's eassumption picks... it might pick H1 (value t) since it's more recent (H1 is above Hv in the list, so more recently introduced). Then ?v := t, and the second goal becomes `t --> ?t'`; there's no hypothesis `t --> _` in this branch (only v --> t2'), so eassumption fails.

Fix: be explicit: `exact (value_no_step v t2' Hv H3)` — but H3 and Hv names might vary. Use a match with ? patterns:

```

match goal with
| Hst : ?a --> ?b |- False => exact (value_no_step a b _ Hst)
end.
```
This requires the `_` (value a) to be found by unification — no, `_` in a term creates an evar that must be resolved... `exact (value_no_step a b _ Hst)` would leave an unresolved evar unless it's inferred. Better: match both hypotheses:

```
match goal with
| Hst : ?a --> ?b, Hval : value ?a |- False => exact (value_no_step a b Hval Hst)
end.
```
This is robust ✓.
